Journal of Quantum Information Science > Vol.16 No.4, December 2026
Diffusion-Operator Qubit Bounds and an Error-Burst Qubit Ceiling for Oracle-Based Quantum Search: A Conditional Resource Estimate
Ying Liu
Department of Engineering Technology, Savannah State University, Savannah, GA, USA.
DOI: 10.4236/jqis.2026.164018   PDF    HTML   XML   7 Downloads   97 Views  
Abstract

Grover’s algorithm searches an unstructured database of N = 2n items in O(N−−√
 ) sequential oracle-and-diffusion rounds, a bound already proven optimal. This paper asks a different question: given realistic fault-tolerant hardware constraints, how large an unstructured search can actually be carried to completion? Using a spacetime-volume resource model—accounting for circuit depth, active and idle qubit exposure, and surface-code error suppression—we first derive an oracle-independent upper bound: the diffusion operator alone, together with the round count fixed by the N−−√
 lower bound, bounds the number of index qubits that a completed search can use to roughly 22 to 53, depending on code distance, a bound that no oracle implementation can loosen, only tighten. We then identify a second, distinct constraint: error bursts, rare correlated fault events with a specific physical origin in cosmic-ray and ambient-radiation exposure, measured directly on current superconducting hardware, are not suppressed by increasing code distance the way ordinary logical error is. This produces an asymptotic ceiling of approximately 50 index qubits that persists regardless of code distance, since the limiting factor becomes a fault mechanism that ordinary error correction does not address. This finding is scoped deliberately: it is a conditional resource estimate tied to one class of hardware’s currently measured, largely unmitigated behavior, not a fundamental limit on quantum computing or a demonstration that this failure mode is physically irreducible—active mitigation research and recent theoretical work indicate the ceiling may relax as hardware and decoding strategies mature. Finally, we show the oracle-independent bound degrades gracefully rather than catastrophically as realistic oracle cost is introduced, via a single-parameter sensitivity analysis.

Keywords

Quantum Computing, Grover’s Algorithm, Oracle, Surface Code, Quantum Error Correction, Error Bursts, Spacetime Volume, Resource Estimation, Fault-Tolerant Quantum Computing

Share and Cite:

FacebookTwitterLinkedInSina WeiboShare
Liu, Y. (2026) Diffusion-Operator Qubit Bounds and an Error-Burst Qubit Ceiling for Oracle-Based Quantum Search: A Conditional Resource Estimate. Journal of Quantum Information Science, 16, 494-513. doi: 10.4236/jqis.2026.164018.
1. Introduction

Grover’s algorithm searches an unstructured database of N items using O(N−−√
 ) sequential oracle-and-diffusion rounds, a quadratic speedup over classical search that is provably optimal [1]-[3]. That optimality is not in question here. What this paper asks instead is a resource question: given realistic constraints on fault-tolerant quantum hardware, how large a search problem can such a deep, sequential algorithm actually be run to completion on?

This question does not reduce to counting logical qubits. A quantum computer with many available logical qubits is not thereby able to run a deep computation to completion—circuit depth, not qubit count, is what a fault-tolerant error budget actually constrains, and the two are only coupled for algorithms whose required depth scales with the size of the problem instance encoded in the qubit register. Unstructured search is the clearest such case: doubling the number of index qubits roughly squares the number of sequential rounds required. This coupling is what makes Grover’s algorithm a useful lens for asking how deep a fault-tolerant computation can practically go, and it is the reason a bound on achievable depth translates, for this algorithm specifically, into a bound on the number of index qubits a completed search can use.

Two separate resource questions arise once this framing is in place, and this paper treats them as genuinely separate. The first is how expensive the search algorithm’s own structure is, independent of any particular oracle: Sections 2 and 3 show that the diffusion operator alone, together with the fixed number of rounds required by the N−−√
 lower bound, sets a resource bound that no oracle implementation can loosen, only tighten—an oracle-independent upper bound that holds regardless of how the oracle is built. Section 7 then asks how much a real oracle’s cost narrows this bound, without committing to any single oracle’s specific implementation.

The second question is what “achievable depth” means once realistic error correction is accounted for, and this is where this paper’s central finding emerges. Standard fault-tolerant resource estimates assume that logical faults occur independently at each qubit, each cycle—an assumption that error-corrected quantum memories are specifically designed to make safe. But this assumption has a measured exception. Superconducting quantum processors are subject to error bursts: rare, transient events, physically traced to cosmic-ray muons and ambient radioactivity striking the chip substrate, that elevate the fault rate across many qubits simultaneously [4] [5]. Sections 4 through 6 show that this failure mode, measured directly on current hardware, is not suppressed by increasing code distance the way ordinary logical error is, and that this asymmetry produces an asymptotic ceiling on the number of index qubits a completed search can use—a ceiling that persists no matter how good the underlying error-correcting code is chosen to be.

It is important to state precisely what kind of claim it is, since the distinction matters throughout. It is not a fundamental limit on quantum computing, nor a bound on how many logical qubits fault-tolerant hardware can support—current and near-term devices already exceed the qubit counts this paper discusses, running algorithms whose depth does not scale the way Grover’s does. It is also not a settled, irreducible physical limit: active mitigation research targets the underlying error-burst rate directly, and separate recent theoretical work indicates that appropriately designed decoding and mitigation strategies may restore favorable code-distance scaling even in the presence of these events [6]. What this paper reports is a conditional resource estimate: given the error-burst rate currently measured on one class of superconducting hardware, and without additional burst-specific mitigation, this is the largest unstructured search Grover’s algorithm could complete. Section 4 discusses this scope explicitly, including which parts of the finding are specific to the cited hardware and which reflect a more general concern for fault-tolerant computation.

The remainder of the paper proceeds as follows. Section 2 develops the resource-accounting model—spacetime volume rather than gate count alone, and the diffusion operator construction this paper adopts. Section 3 establishes the oracle-independent upper bound and proves that no real oracle can loosen it. Sections 4 through 6 introduce error bursts, incorporate them into the resource model, and derive the resulting asymptotic ceiling. Section 7 discusses how a real oracle’s cost tightens the bound established in Section 3. Section 8 explains the reasons behind the bounds and ceiling. Section 9 concludes.

2. Model and Upper Bound Equation

To compute a resource bound on the number of logical qubits usable by a given algorithm, several assumptions about the algorithm’s structure are required. An algorithm generally depends on both an oracle operator and a diffusion operator. The diffusion operator alone, however, imposes a bound on the number of logical qubits usable in a completed search, independent of the oracle’s implementation. We derive this bound first (Section 3) and introduce the oracle’s effect afterward (Section 7). Throughout, we depend on a number of explicit assumptions, listed in full at the end of this section (§2.8); several of these may shift as hardware continues to improve.

2.1. Logical Error Budget

Under active quantum error correction, a logical qubit’s usable lifetime [7] [8] is not set by the raw physical decoherence time T2—continuous syndrome extraction and correction is designed precisely to outlast T2. The relevant constraint instead is how much computation (gate count, depth, or volume) can be performed before the accumulated probability of an uncorrected logical fault exceeds an acceptable threshold for the whole computation, which will be discussed in the next subsection.

2.2. Resource Definitions

We distinguish three resource quantities:

Circuit depth D: the length of the critical path—the number of sequential time layers, assuming maximal parallelism within each layer.

Total logical gate count G: the sum of all gate operations across all layers. A layer with w parallel gates contributes 1 to D but w to G.

Total logical spacetime volume V: the number of (logical qubit) × (QEC cycle) pairs exposed to error over the computation,

V≈nactive×D
(1)

where D is the circuit depth, nactive includes every logical qubit present—index qubits and ancilla—since under active surface-code QEC, idle qubits still require syndrome extraction every cycle and accrue logical error whether or not a gate acts on them that cycle.

Failure probability accumulates with spacetime volume, not logical gate count alone—a qubit sitting idle for many cycles is exposed to error just as a qubit being actively gated is. All resource accounting below is therefore done in terms of V.

Fault-tolerant quantum computation works by continuously monitoring every logical qubit, every cycle, whether or not that qubit is being gated at that moment. The whole computation can be pictured as a two-dimensional grid: one axis is which logical qubit (index qubits, plus any ancilla present), the other is which QEC cycle. Every cell in this grid—one qubit, for one cycle—carries some small, independent probability of an uncorrected logical fault. This grid is exactly what “spacetime volume” means: V is the number of cells in it.

2.3. Diffusion Operator Construction

The diffusion operator

D=2|ψ⟩⟨ψ|−I
(2)

can be realized with either a minimal-ancilla linear construction (depth O(n), O(1) ancilla) or an ancilla-tree construction (depth O(logn), O(n) ancilla)—see Appendix A for both constructions and the derivation below. Comparing spacetime volume across Grover’s K(n) rounds shows the ancilla-tree construction has lower total volume for n ≳ 16, which covers the entire problem-size range this paper addresses. We adopt it throughout:

Ddiff(n)≈2⋅log2n
(3)

nactive,diff(n)≈2n
(4)

conditional on sufficient physical qubits being available for the O(n) diffusion ancilla.

2.4. Memory and Active Logical Error Assumption

Within the diffusion operator’s window, each logical qubit at each cycle is either idle or being gated, and the two cases carry different logical error rates. The memory rate, Pmem, applies to idle cycles and follows the standard surface-code suppression formula (§2.5), sourced from quantum-memory experiments [5]. Any cycle in which a qubit participates in a gate incurs the higher rate, Pgate:

Pgate=κ⋅Pmem
(5)

with κ ≈ 1.25 - 1.4 drawn from circuit-level lattice-surgery studies of logical CNOT and merge/split operations relative to memory baselines; we adopt κ = 1.3 as a representative point estimate.

Rather than tracking exactly which cycles are idle and which are gated within the diffusion operator’s window, we consider two simple bracketing choices: pricing every counted qubit-cycle at the lower rate, Pmem (equivalent to κ = 1), or at the higher rate, Pgate. Because the ancilla-tree construction (§2.3) is densely gated for nearly its entire O(log n) depth, we adopt the latter, more conservative choice throughout:

Peff≡Pgate
(6)

for the effective per-cycle rate (effective fault probability per logical-qubit/QEC-cycle exposure) used in all spacetime-volume calculations from here on. Because κ is close to 1, these two bracketing choices are close together: solving for the maximum index-qubit count under Peff = Pmem versus Peff = Pgate shifts the result by less than one qubit across the entire range this paper considers. The simpler, uniform, and conservative choice therefore costs little.

2.5. Surface-Code Logical Error Rate

Pmem follows the standard surface-code suppression formula [7] [8]:

Pmem=A⋅(ppth)d+12
(7)

where

p is the physical two-qubit gate error rate [7] [8],

pth ≈ 0.01 is the code threshold [9]-[11],

d is the code distance, and

A ≈ 0.1 - 1 is an empirical prefactor.

Physical qubits per logical qubit scale as b ≈ 2d2 for a standard surface code—some 2025-2026 architectures already beat this ratio substantially (e.g., ~2:1 physical-to-logical overhead reported for some trapped-ion and neutral-atom codes) [5] [12], so 2d2 is a conservative, not universal, estimate.

Google’s Willow processor has demonstrated a below-threshold, distance-7 surface code quantum memory with per-cycle logical error rate 0.143% [5]; IBM heavy-hex architectures have separately demonstrated subthreshold scaling to anisotropic distance-(5,3)/(3,5) codes [12], on a native connectivity that does not natively match the surface code lattice. Both are broadly consistent with p ≈ 10−3 as a representative near-term rate. The code distances used in this paper’s sensitivity analysis (d = 11 - 21) exceed what has been experimentally demonstrated to date (d ≤ 7); the resulting estimates should be read as near-term projections under continued scaling, not results already achieved.

2.6. Grover’s Resource Requirement

Searching N = 2n items requires

K(n)=(π/4)⋅2n/2
(8)

oracle-and-diffusion rounds [2]. In this section, oracle depth and oracle ancilla count are both set to zero—a “no-oracle” idealization, addressed explicitly in Section 7. Per-round depth and active-qubit count are then given entirely by the diffusion operator (Equation (3) and Equation (4)), and total spacetime volume across all K(n) rounds is:

V(n)=K(n)⋅nactive,diff(n)⋅Ddiff(n)
(9)

From Equation (3) and Equation (4),

V(n)=π⋅n⋅log2(n)⋅2n/2
(10)

2.7. Upper Bound Equation for the Maximum Index-Qubit Count

For a fault-tolerant quantum computation, the appropriate exponent (Circuit depth D, Total logical gate count G, Total logical spacetime volume V) in the fault-free probability model depends on the unit associated with the effective fault probability Peff. When Peff denotes the probability of a logical fault per logical-qubit exposure during one QEC cycle, the relevant number of independent fault opportunities is the total logical spacetime volume V, defined as the number of (logical-qubit, QEC-cycle) pairs over the computation.

The computation succeeds only if every cell in this grid comes through clean. With V independent cells, each with fault probability Peff (the effective fault probability per logical-qubit/QEC-cycle exposure), the probability that all of them are fault-free is

1−ε=(1−Peff)V
(11)

So the probability of at least one fault anywhere—a computational failure—is

ε=1−(1−Peff)V(n)
(12)

For small Peff (Peff ranges from ~10−7 to ~10−12 across our code distances), this is closely approximated by the first term of its expansion:

ε≈V(n)⋅Peff
(13)

A simpler alternative would set a budget on total gate count alone, ignoring qubits that are present but not being directly gated at a given cycle. This implicitly treats an idle qubit as carrying no risk—which is not physically accurate (§2.4). Under active surface-code QEC, an idle qubit is still tracked by continuous syndrome extraction every cycle, and still carries its own, smaller only by a factor of κ, chance of an uncorrected fault each cycle; that continuous monitoring is precisely why quantum error correction works at all.

In Grover’s algorithm, this matters a great deal: the n index qubits must remain coherent and error-corrected across the full K(n)-round computation, but within any single round—and even within a single diffusion operator’s own O(logn)-depth construction, where most qubits sit waiting at any given layer while only a subset are combined—they are directly gated for only a small fraction of the elapsed cycles. K(n) itself is enormous across the range this paper considers, see Equation (8). Counting gate operations alone assigns zero risk to all the remaining idle cycles; counting spacetime volume assigns each of them their proper share. This is an important contributing factor to the low value of the bound proposed in this paper.

Setting the total spacetime volume against the failure budget therefore gives the Upper Bound Equation:

ε≈π⋅n⋅log2(n)⋅2n/2⋅κ⋅A⋅(ppth)d+12
(14)

Equation (14) is transcendental in n for the upper bound of logical qubits. Section 3 solves it numerically for representative code distances.

2.8. Assumptions

The bound derived from Equation (14) depends on the following explicit assumptions, several of which may shift as hardware and error-correction methods improve:

1) A single marked item, black-box oracle access, one run without repetition, target success probability 1/2.

2) Grover round count K(n) per Equation (8).

3) Diffusion operator realized via the ancilla-tree construction (Appendix A), valid for n ≳ 16.

4) Oracle depth and ancilla set to zero in this section (relaxed in Section 7).

5) Surface-code memory error model, Equation (7), with p ≈ 10−3, pth = 0.01, A = 0.1, drawn from near-term hardware demonstrations at d ≤ 7; code distances d = 11 - 21 used in Section 3 exceed what has been demonstrated.

6) Two-rate active/idle error model, κ ≈ 1.3 (§2.4), applied uniformly and conservatively.

7) Overall failure budget ε = 0.1, a chosen target rather than a derived quantity.

8) Per-cycle faults assumed independent, except where explicitly revisited in Section 4.

9) Physical-qubit overhead per logical qubit modeled as b ≈ 2d2, a conservative estimate relative to some current architectures.

3. Oracle-Independent Upper Bounds

Section 2 built the entire resource model under one explicit idealization: that the oracle contributes zero additional depth and requires no additional ancilla qubits. That’s not a realistic assumption—every real oracle does some work. This is what makes this bound useful rather than merely illustrative, it applies to any oracle.

The bound on logical qubits in Section 2 can be solved once, without ever having to pin down what a real oracle costs, and the resulting number is not an approximation of the true bound—it is provably at least as large as the true bound for any real algorithm. Any real oracle adds depth and ancilla, which can only increase the spacetime volume, and the number of rounds K(n) is fixed by the N−−√
 lower bound [2], [3]; the bound computed here is therefore at least as large as the true bound for any oracle. Section 7 discusses what happens once a real oracle’s cost is added back in; everything in this section describes the best case that no real implementation can exceed.

Using the numerical values of Section 2.8 and solving Upper Bound Equation (Equation (14)) numerically for representative code distances gives Table 1.

Table 1. Oracle-independent upper bound for logical qubit at each code distance.

Code distance, d

nmax (oracle-free)

11

22.5

15

34.2

21

52.6

These are the largest problem sizes the diffusion operator alone, at each code distance, can carry to completion within the stated failure budget—and, by the argument above, no real oracle can push any of these numbers higher.

A remark on the κ simplification: Section 2.4 priced every qubit-cycle in the diffusion operator’s window at the higher of two possible rates, Pgate = κ·Pmem with κ ≈ 1.3 (Equation (5)), and claimed this shifts the result by less than one qubit relative to using the lower rate, Pmem, throughout. Solving Equation (14) both ways confirms this: at d = 11, 15, and 21, the two choices differ by 0.65, 0.68, and 0.71 qubits respectively—under one qubit at every code distance considered, and the gap grows only slowly with d. The simplification made in Section 2.4 costs very little in exchange for not having to track which specific cycles are idle and which are gated.

4. Error Burst

The correlated fault this paper’s asymptotic ceiling depends on has a specific, well-characterized physical origin—not leakage, not calibration drift, and not the static crosstalk familiar from superconducting two-qubit gates. When a cosmic-ray muon or gamma radiation from ambient radioactivity strikes the chip substrate, it deposits substantial energy—100 keV to 1 MeV, far above the ~350 μeV needed to break a single Cooper pair [4]. This energy radiates outward as high-energy phonons that propagate through the substrate and break Cooper pairs across a wide area of the chip, producing a transient avalanche of quasiparticles. These quasiparticles tunnel across the Josephson junctions at the heart of each affected qubit, driving simultaneous energy decay across many qubits at once—precisely the correlated, code-distance-resistant failure mode this paper’s model in Section 5 depends on.

It is worth being precise about what this paper’s “ceiling” is and is not. It is not a directly observed computational limit: no computation of the length this paper considers has been run and shown to saturate at this point. Nor is it derived from first-principles physics alone. Physics explains the mechanism—that cosmic-ray muons and ambient gamma radiation will occasionally strike the chip substrate and generate a burst of quasiparticles—but the specific rate at which this happens is an empirically measured quantity, not one physics alone predicts a value for. The measured rate used here, roughly one event per hour, comes from dedicated, much shorter burst-detection experiments [4] [5], not from running a search algorithm to its predicted limit. What this paper reports is a projection: a real, physically measured per-event rate carried through the resource-accounting model of Sections 2 - 3, via the same reasoning used throughout. It is a ceiling produced by a specific mathematical model, resting on a genuine physical mechanism together with an empirically measured input.

1) Efforts that improve the rate itself

Mitigation research targeting the burst rate directly is active and has already demonstrated partial success: physical die separation—fabricating the qubit array across two adjacent, galvanically isolated substrates—substantially reduces, though does not eliminate, correlated events spanning both dies. Phonon downconversion using islands of lower-gap superconducting material [13], quasiparticle traps, and Josephson-junction gap engineering have each shown partial suppression of burst severity or frequency. Each of these approaches works by lowering how often, or how severely, a burst event occurs in the first place.

2) The rate as currently modeled is independent of code distance

The burst rate itself does not depend on the code distance d chosen for error correction: unlike ordinary logical error, which shrinks exponentially with d, nothing about increasing d changes how often a cosmic-ray event strikes the chip. But this does not mean the code’s resilience to that fixed rate is similarly fixed. A dedicated recent study of exactly this question [6] shows that given appropriate mitigation limiting burst duration and standard decoding adapted to handle it, surface-code memory time can grow arbitrarily long as code size increases even in the presence of error bursts, in the regimes they study—and at the architectural level, distributed error-correction schemes have been proposed specifically to tolerate chip-wide events by spreading a logical qubit’s physical qubits across separated dies [14].

The rate itself is a hardware-specific input, not a universal constant. Equation (16) applies this single-chip rate to the modeled machine regardless of its size; a machine spanning many chips would be exposed to a higher overall rate, which lowers the ceiling—it reflects the measured cosmic-ray and radioactivity flux at one device’s location and packaging, scaled by chip area; a different device, shielding arrangement, or underground facility would measure a materially different rate.

This leaves a two-part answer to whether this paper’s result is general or hardware-specific, and the two parts should not be conflated. The qualitative phenomenon—a rare, transient, spatially correlated spike in error rate defeating the independent-fault assumption standard quantum error correction relies on—is not unique to superconducting hardware or to cosmic rays. The same 2026 study frames this as a general class and notes that trapped-ion and neutral-atom platforms face an analogous risk from large, correlated deviations in shared global control fields—an entirely different physical mechanism, but a structurally similar failure mode. In that sense, the concern this paper raises is plausibly general to fault-tolerant quantum computing. The specific numbers are not: the rate in Equation (16) below, and the resulting n ≈ 50 ceiling in Section 6, are tied to one measured device’s currently reported, largely unmitigated behavior, and should be read as an illustration of what today’s hardware implies under this paper’s model, not a claim about fault-tolerant computing’s ultimate limits.

5. The Upper Bound Equation with Error Burst

Acharya et al. [5] report the error burst rate as roughly once per hour, on the same device whose 1.1-microsecond QEC cycle time is used throughout this paper. Converting between the two: one hour contains

3600 s/(1.1×10−6 s)≈3.27×109 cycles

which we round to 3 × 109 for Equation (16) below. This rounding is deliberately conservative: using the more precise 3.27 × 109 figure would very slightly raise the asymptotic ceiling in Section 6, from n ≈ 50.0 to n ≈ 50.3—an 8% adjustment to the rate that moves the headline result by under a third of a qubit, well within the precision this paper claims anywhere else.

We can fold this into the same reasoning used to derive Equations (11)-(13). The total number of cycles the diffusion-only computation requires is:

Dtotal(n)=K(n)⋅Ddiff(n)
(15)

Treating error bursts as occurring at a small, roughly constant per-cycle rate,

pburst≈1/(3×109)
(16)

the chance of encountering at least one during a computation of Dtotal(n) cycles is, by the same reasoning that produced Equation (13):

εburst(n)≈Dtotal(n)⋅pburst
(17)

Inserting Equation (15) and Equation (16),

εburst(n)=K(n)⋅Ddiff(n)/(3×109)
(18)

Inserting Equation (3) and Equation (8),

εburst(n)=(π/2)⋅log2(n)⋅2n/2/(3×109)
(19)

Because this failure mode arises from a different physical mechanism than the ordinary per-cycle error already modeled in Equation (14), the two risks can be combined the same way ordinary faults were combined across many qubit-cycles: by adding their probabilities.

εtotal(n)≈εgate(n)+εburst(n)
(20)

where εgate(n) is exactly the quantity solved via Equation (14) earlier. Inserting Equation (14) and Equation (19) into Equation (20) gives the Upper Bound Equation with Error Burst:

εtotal(n)≈π⋅log2(n)⋅2n/2⋅[n⋅κ⋅A⋅(ppth)d+12+16×109]
(21)

The second term is genuinely illuminating. Both terms share the common factor π·log2(n)·2n/2, and factoring it out separates the two failure mechanisms cleanly inside the brackets: the first term, n·κ·A·(p/pth)(d+1)/2, is the gate-error contribution—it shrinks toward zero as d grows, since larger code distance suppresses it exponentially. The second term, 1/(6 × 109), is the error-burst contribution—a fixed constant, completely unaffected by d.

This indicates that increasing code distance stops helping at some point: the bracket can never shrink below 1/(6 × 109) no matter how large d gets. As shown in Section 6, it is this second term that produces the n ≈ 50 asymptotic ceiling.

Solving this combined equation for n, at the same budget εtotal = 0.1, gives a materially different picture at the high end of the range from Section 3 in Table 2.

Table 2. Oracle-independent upper bound for logical qubits at each code distance, considering the error burst effect.

d

nmax

(Gate error only)

nmax

(gate error + error bursts)

11

22.5

22.5 (unaffected)

15

34.2

34.2 (unaffected)

21

52.6

49.1 (7% change)

At the two smaller code distances, error-burst risk is negligible next to ordinary gate error, and the gate-only bound from Section 3 is essentially unchanged. At d = 21, it is not: nearly all of the failure budget at the joint solution is being spent on error-burst risk rather than ordinary error. This raises a natural question, addressed in Section 6: what happens as code distance keeps increasing—does the bound keep improving indefinitely, or does something else take over? The next section discusses an important concept, the asymptotic ceiling of logical qubits.

6. Asymptotic Ceiling of Logical Qubits

Ordinary gate error can, in principle, be suppressed arbitrarily by choosing a sufficiently large code distance—the surface-code formula (Equation 7) shrinks exponentially in d. Error-burst risk cannot: Equation (19) shows εburst(n) has no dependence on d at all. This asymmetry is the source of Asymptotic Ceiling Equation in this section.

It is visible directly in the factored form of Equation (21):

εtotal(n)≈π⋅log2(n)⋅2n/2⋅[n⋅κ⋅A⋅(ppth)d+12+16×109]

As d grows arbitrarily large, the first term inside the brackets shrinks toward zero, but the second term, 1/(6 × 109), stays fixed. The bracket can never fall below this floor no matter how large d becomes—meaning εtotal(n) can never be driven below π·log2(n)·2n/2/(6 × 109) by improving the code distance alone.

Taking the d→∞ limit, in which the gate-error term vanishes, and setting the entire failure budget equal to error-burst risk alone, gives:

εtotal(n) =π⋅log2(n)⋅2n/2/(6×109)
(22)

Solving the Asymptotic Ceiling Equation (22) for n gives n ≈ 50.0. This is the asymptotic ceiling: no matter how large a code distance is chosen, this computation cannot exceed roughly 50 index qubits at the stated budget. Past this point, the limiting factor is no longer ordinary error correction at all—it is a fault mechanism that error correction, as currently understood for this hardware, does not suppress.

7. Discussion of Oracles

Section 3 established that any real oracle can only lower the bounds derived in Sections 3 - 5, never raise them: a real oracle’s added depth and ancilla can only add to total resource cost, never subtract from it. This section asks how much lower, without committing to any single oracle’s specific cost, since an unstructured black-box oracle has no universal implementation and no single number could speak for all of them.

Rather than model a particular oracle circuit, we introduce one parameter, γ ≥ 1, representing the ratio of the round’s total resource cost—diffusion operator and oracle combined—to the diffusion operator’s cost alone:

Dround(n)=Doracle(n)+Ddiff(n)=γ⋅Ddiff(n)
(23)

γ = 1 recovers the zero-additional-cost baseline of Sections 3 - 5 exactly. γ = 2 represents an oracle whose own contribution roughly doubles the round’s total cost, compared to diffusion alone; or oracle cost = diffusion cost. A larger γ represents progressively more expensive oracles. For simplicity, the total active-qubit footprint is scaled by the same factor as the round’s total depth:

nactive,round(n)=γ⋅nactive,diff(n)
(24)

This is a deliberate simplification, not a claim about how real oracle circuits behave—a real oracle’s depth and ancilla requirements need not scale identically. It is chosen because it makes the resulting sensitivity transparent and traceable to a single, explicitly stated assumption, rather than to an unstated or unjustified circuit model.

Since spacetime volume is the product of active-qubit count and depth, scaling both by γ scales volume by γ2:

Vtotal(n,γ)=K(n)⋅nactive,round(n)⋅Dround(n)=γ2⋅V(n)
(25)

where V(n) is the diffusion-only volume from Section 2. The corresponding gate-error contribution scales the same way,

εgate,total(n,γ)=γ2⋅εgate(n)
(26)

Error-burst exposure, by contrast, depends only on total depth (Equation (15)), so it scales linearly rather than quadratically:

εburst,total(n,γ)=γ⋅εburst(n)
(27)

Combining both terms gives the generalized failure budget:

εtotal(n,γ)≈γ2⋅εgate(n)+γ⋅εburst(n)
(28)

The Upper Bound Equation (Equation (14)) extends:

ε≈π⋅γ2⋅n⋅log2(n)⋅2n/2⋅κ⋅A⋅(ppth)d+12
(29)

The Upper Bound Equation (Equation (21)) with Error Burst extends:

εtotal(n,γ)≈π⋅log2(n)⋅2n/2⋅[γ2⋅n⋅κ⋅A⋅(ppth)d+12+γ6×109]
(30)

The Asymptotic Ceiling Equation (Equation (22)) extends

εtotal(n,γ)=π⋅γ⋅log2(n)⋅2n/2/(6×109)
(31)

Solving Equation (30) for n at fixed γ, across the same code distances used throughout, gives Table 3.

Table 3. Maximum index qubits as a function of oracle cost multiplier γ.

d

γ = 1

γ = 2

γ = 5

γ = 10

11

22.5

19.1

14.8

11.8

15

34.2

30.6

25.9

22.5

21

49.1

46.5

42.7

39.5

The bound degrades gracefully as oracle cost increases rather than collapsing sharply: even a tenfold increase in per-round cost (γ = 10, a deliberately pessimistic scenario) reduces the d = 21 bound from 49.1 to 39.5—a real effect, but one that leaves the paper’s central conclusion intact rather than overturning it. This is the sense in which the qubit bound is oracle-independent in the strong sense established in Section 3: it does not require knowing the oracle’s cost to be useful, and remains informative across a wide range of possible oracle costs.

One resource dimension this section’s model does not capture is worth naming explicitly rather than glossing over: real oracles typically require non-Clifford operations (Toffoli gates decomposed into T-gates, for instance), which in a fault-tolerant surface-code implementation are supplied by dedicated magic-state distillation circuits with their own resource footprint, separate from the depth and ancilla accounting above. Whether this cost is dominant is itself an unsettled question in the literature: it was long treated as the primary bottleneck of fault-tolerant computation following its original proposal [15], but more recent optimized distillation protocols have substantially reduced this overhead—in one detailed worked example, to roughly 2% of total physical qubits [16]. Given this is contested and architecture-dependent, we do not attempt to fold a specific magic-state cost into γ here, since doing so would require committing to a particular oracle’s non-Clifford gate count—precisely the kind of oracle-specific assumption this section has aimed to avoid throughout. A fully worked treatment of non-Clifford resource cost is left as an explicit direction for future work, rather than folded into the present estimate.

8. Discussion

8.1. Depth Wall

The achievable circuit depth of a quantum computation is fundamentally limited by the overhead of Quantum Error Correction (QEC) [7] [8], regardless of how many logical qubits the underlying hardware provides. We call this fixed, assumption-dependent limit on achievable depth the Depth Wall.

For some quantum algorithms, this depth wall does not translate directly into a limit on the number of qubits the algorithm can use, because depth and qubit count are largely independent design choices. But for a specific and important class of algorithms—those whose required depth scales with the size of the problem instance encoded in the qubit register—the Depth Wall translates directly into a resource bound on qubit count.

Grover’s algorithm [1] [2] requires Θ(N−−√
 ) sequential oracle-and-diffusion rounds to search a database of N = 2n items, so the number of index qubits, n, and the required circuit depth are locked together; a limit on circuit depth therefore limits the number of qubits, n, as well.

Beyond a certain depth, an independent constraint enters. As shown in Sections 4 - 6, error bursts impose their own limit on achievable depth—one that, unlike ordinary logical error, is not relieved by choosing a larger code distance.

The factors that fix the Depth Wall—the Θ(N−−√
 ) amplitude-amplification requirement, the per-round resource cost of the diffusion operator (and, per Section 7, the oracle), the per-gate error suppression of surface-code QEC, and error bursts—together place a practical limit on the largest unstructured search problem, in terms of index qubits n, that can be run to completion within a fixed computational error budget.

8.2. A Tighter Constraint than Hardware Qubit Count

Because this limit follows from the depth requirement of the algorithm itself, it applies independently of how many logical qubits the underlying hardware provides. Larger quantum computers, in terms of more logical qubits, will not help in these cases, making this resource bound a tighter restriction than the underlying hardware’s qubit count.

Recent public hardware reports already describe systems with several dozen logical qubits and roadmaps targeting hundreds by the end of the decade. This is fully consistent with the estimates above, because none of those demonstrations are running deep Θ(N−−√
 ) Grover searches—they are running shallow entangling circuits [17], Bell-state [17] and GHZ-state preparations [18] [19], Bernstein-Vazirani-type algorithms [20], or error-correction memory experiments [5], all of which have depth requirements that grow slowly, or not at all, with qubit count.

Hardware logical qubit count and the depth-derived index-qubit bound are separate axes of scaling, and they are currently improving at very different rates. The practical implication is that near-term and early-fault-tolerant machines will likely have enough logical qubits to index very large unstructured search spaces well before they have enough usable depth to actually search them—Grover’s algorithm, and any other algorithm with genuinely problem-size-dependent sequential depth requirements, will lag behind raw qubit-count progress for exactly this reason.

9. Conclusions

This paper asked a resource question about unstructured search rather than a query-complexity one: given realistic fault-tolerant hardware constraints, how large a Grover search can actually be carried to completion in terms of the number of logical qubits? The answer developed across the paper has two independent parts, and neither reduces to a simple qubit count.

The first part concerns the algorithm’s own structure. Section 2 and 3 showed that the diffusion operator alone, together with the round count fixed by the N−−√
 lower bound, establishes a resource bound that no oracle implementation can loosen—an oracle-independent upper bound of roughly 22 to 53 index qubits across the code distances considered, depending on the surface code’s suppression of ordinary gate error. The Upper Bound Equation is derived and solving this equation will produce this oracle-independent upper bound as a function of code distance. Section 7 showed this bound degrades gracefully, not catastrophically, as real oracle cost is introduced: even a tenfold increase in per-round cost relative to the diffusion operator alone left the paper’s central conclusions intact. The Upper Bound Equation with Oracle is derived and solving this equation will produce the upper bound as function of code distance with oracle effects.

The second, more consequential part concerns a failure mode that standard resource estimates do not typically account for. Sections 4 through 6 showed that error bursts—rare, transient, spatially correlated fault events with a specific, well-characterized physical origin in cosmic-ray and ambient-radiation exposure—are not suppressed by increasing code distance the way ordinary logical error is, at least under currently reported, largely unmitigated hardware operation. This asymmetry produces an asymptotic ceiling: no matter how large a code distance is chosen, the computation modeled in this paper cannot exceed roughly 50 index qubits at the stated error budget, because the limiting factor stops being ordinary error correction entirely. The Asymptotic Ceiling Equation is derived and solving this equation will produce this ceiling, 50.

This finding should be read for precisely what it is, not more. It is a conditional resource estimate, not a fundamental limit on quantum computing or on the number of logical qubits fault-tolerant hardware can support—current devices already exceed the qubit counts discussed here, running algorithms whose required depth does not couple to problem size the way Grover’s does. It is not a proven, irreducible physical bound: mitigation research targeting the error-burst rate directly is active and has shown real partial success, and separate, dedicated theoretical work indicates that appropriately designed mitigation and decoding may restore favorable code-distance scaling even in the presence of these events. And it is only partly general: the qualitative concern—that rare, correlated failures can defeat the independent-fault assumption standard error correction relies on, in a way ordinary code-distance scaling does not address—plausibly extends across quantum computing platforms, but the specific numbers reported here are tied to one class of superconducting hardware’s currently measured, largely unmitigated behavior.

Two limitations are worth stating explicitly as directions for future work rather than as gaps quietly left unaddressed. First, this paper’s resource model accounts for depth, gate count, and idle-versus-active logical error, but does not cost the non-Clifford operations that any real oracle, and the diffusion operator’s own ancilla-tree construction, are likely to require; magic-state distillation cost is a separate resource dimension this paper has deliberately left unresolved, since folding it in would require committing to a specific, unjustifiable oracle circuit. Second, the error-burst rate used throughout is a single hardware-specific measurement; as mitigation strategies mature and as other qubit modalities are characterized for their own correlated-failure mechanisms, the numerical ceiling reported here should be expected to shift, likely upward, without the underlying methodology changing.

The broader point this paper makes is not about Grover’s algorithm specifically. Any algorithm whose required circuit depth scales with the size of the problem it solves will eventually run long enough, in wall-clock QEC cycles, to become exposed to rare correlated failure modes that ordinary error-correction scaling does not address. As fault-tolerant hardware matures and deep algorithms become a realistic target rather than a theoretical one, resource estimates for such algorithms may need to account for this class of failure explicitly, alongside the gate-count and code-distance considerations that have so far dominated the literature.

Appendix A: Diffusion Operator Construction

Two constructions implement the diffusion operator, D = 2|ψ⟩⟨ψ| − I, applied once per Grover round; only the circuit realizing it differs. The number of rounds, K(n) = (π/4)·2n/2, is fixed by amplitude amplification and is identical for both constructions. This appendix isolates the diffusion operator alone, independent of the oracle operator.

Any reflection about a prepared state |ψ⟩ can be implemented via the identity [21]:

Rψ=2|ψ⟩⟨ψ|−I=U(2|0⟩⟨0|−I)U†
(A.1)

where U prepares the state, U|0⟩ = |ψ⟩. Rather than implementing |ψ⟩⟨ψ| directly, the reflection proceeds in three steps: 1) uncompute |ψ⟩ to |0⟩ via U†; 2) flip the phase, |0n⟩ → −|0n⟩; 3) recompute |0⟩ back to |ψ⟩ via U. So Rψ = U∙(phase flip)∙U†. Steps 1 and 3 cost (U†) and (U) respectively; step 2 is a multi-controlled-Z gate on n qubits, whose depth and ancilla requirements depend on which construction is chosen—the subject of this appendix.

A.1. Two Constructions

Barenco et al. [22] give two constructions for the n-fold controlled phase flip with different depth/ancilla trade-offs:

Option 1 (minimal ancilla). A single borrowed ancilla qubit and a chain of Toffoli-type gates, each depending structurally on the previous step’s output. Depth scales linearly with the number of controls:

Ddiff,1(n)≈n
(A.2)

nancilla≈O(1)
(A.3)

nactive,1(n)≈n
(A.4)

Option 2 (ancilla tree). An (n − 2)-ancilla balanced binary tree computes the n-fold AND in depth log2n by letting independent branches proceed concurrently, applies a single controlled-Z, then uncomputes the tree:

Ddiff,2(n)≈2⋅log2n
(A.5)

Nancilla≈n−2
(A.6)

nactive,2(n)≈2n
(A.7)

These are genuinely different circuits, not two schedulings of the same one: Option 1’s chain cannot be parallelized below O(n) depth, since each link depends on the last; Option 2 trades n − 2 additional qubits for the ability to compute independent branches at once.

A.2. Spacetime Volume

§2.2, total logical spacetime volume V is:

V=K(n)⋅nactive(n)⋅Ddiff(n)
(A.8)

From which,

V1(n)=(π/4)⋅2n/2⋅n⋅n=(π/4)⋅n2⋅2n/2
(A.9)

V2(n)=(π/4)⋅2n/2⋅2n⋅2log2n=π⋅n⋅log2(n)⋅2n/2
(A.10)

A.3. Selection

To compute crossover,

V2(n)/V1(n)=4log2(n)/n
(A.11)

Setting this ratio to 1 and solving 4log2(n) = n gives an exact crossover at n = 16 (4·log216 = 16). For n < 16, Option 1 has lower spacetime volume; for n > 16, Option 2 dominates, with the advantage widening as n grows (see Table A1).

Table A1. V2/V1 at representative problem sizes.

n

4log2(n)/n

Favored option

10

1.33

Option 1

16

1.00

crossover

25

0.74

Option 2

37

0.56

Option 2

55

0.42

Option 2

Because n ≥ 16 spans the entire regime this paper addresses, Option 2 is adopted throughout the main text as the default diffusion-operator construction, conditional on sufficient ancilla being available.

Journal of Quantum Information Science > Vol.16 No.3, September 2026
Bio-Quantum Evolution of the Human Brain
Jianzhong Zhao
Department of Geophysics, Yunnan University, Kunming, China.
DOI: 10.4236/jqis.2026.163013   PDF    HTML   XML   36 Downloads   211 Views  
Abstract

The human brain evolves generation by generation. In the present paper I discover, by means of theoretical research into the process of gene transmitting and inheriting of a typical family (a family without loss of its any genes), that the bio-quantum computational speed (the Grover Iterations per second) of mankind increases in the human hereditary process. That means that the ability of the human brain (the central processing unit of biological information and controller of biological process of humans) to control bio-quantum computation strengthens or develops in the human hereditary process. This is the bio-quantum evolution of the human brain. Thus, this work explores the bio-quantum mechanical nature of human brain evolution.

Keywords

Modern Theory of Genetics, Modern Theory of Evolution of the Human Brain, Quantum Biology, Bio-Quantum Computation, Bio-Quantum Evolution of the Human Brain, Law of Evolution of Human Bio-Quantum Intelligence, Bio-Quantum Selection

Share and Cite:

FacebookTwitterLinkedInSina WeiboShare
Zhao, J. (2026) Bio-Quantum Evolution of the Human Brain. Journal of Quantum Information Science, 16, 387-397. doi: 10.4236/jqis.2026.163013.
1. Introduction

Exploration of biological evolution is an important and significant subject of biology, genetics and anthropology. Modern theories of human brain evolution, based on modern genetic theories, have been developed. Volume of researches on human brain evolution, in terms of development of human genes, DNA and genome, has been published.

Enard, W. et al. study molecular evolution of a gene related to development of human speech and language [1]. Dorus, S. et al. examined the evolution of genes involved in human nervous system development [2]. Evans, P. D. et al. find that an important brain gene, Microcephalin (MCPH1) regulating brain size, has continued to evolve adaptively in modern humans [3]. Mekel-Bobrov, N. et al. find that one genetic variant of ASPM, a specific regulator of brain size in humans, arose merely about 5800 years ago and has since swept to high frequency under strong positive selection, suggesting that the human brain is still undergoing rapid adaptive evolution [4]. Results of Prabhakar, S. et al. suggest that widespread cis-regulatory changes in human evolution may have contributed to unique features of development and function of the human brain [5]. Pollard, K.S. et al. show that the ability to compare our genome to that of our closest relative, the chimpanzee, presents new approaches to link genetic and phenotypic changes in the evolution of the human brain [6]. McLean, C.Y. et al. search for putative regulatory mutations specific to the human lineage by looking for sequences deleted in the human genome, illustrated by means of two examples, one of which affects brain size [7]. Paar, V. et al. identify two particular intragene repeat structures of noncoding human DNA, genomic patterns underlying the evolution of the human brain and its emergent advanced cognitive capabilities [8]. The results of Charrier, C. et al. show that inhibition of SRGAP2 function by its human-specific paralogs has contributed to the evolution of the human neocortex and plays an important role during human brain development [9]. Somel, M. et al. review a strategy to yield some of the first hints about the mechanisms of human cognition [10]. Geschwind, DH and Rakic, P. describe how advances in neurobiology, associated with those in genetics, provide insights into the evolutionary mechanisms in the human cerebrum to understand the emergence of human higher cognition [11]. Boyd, J. L. et al. conclude that changes in HARE5 function unique to humans alter the cell-cycle dynamics of a critical population of stem cells during corticogenesis and may underlie some distinctive anatomical features of the human brain [12]. Florio, M. et al. find that human-specific gene ARHGAP11B may have contributed to evolutionary expansion of human neocortex [13]. Fiddes, I.T. et al. Discover that the emergence of human-specific NOTCH2NL genes may have contributed to the rapid evolution of the larger human neocortex [14]. Heide, M. et al. show that the human-specific gene ARHGAP11B drives changes in development in the nonhuman primate marmoset that reflect the changes in evolution that characterize human neocortical development [15]. Trujillo et al. conclude, in their article, that the reintroduction of an ancestral amino acid substitution in the protein NOVA1 drastically alters the development of brain organoids. The comment of Maricic, T. et al. shows that cell lines used by the authors carry heterozygous deletions of the target DNA sequence, providing another plausible explanation for the effects observed [16]. The comment of Herai, R. H. et al. shows that the “putative Neanderthal variant” of TKTL1 is present in modern human backgrounds, disputing the argument that this genetic variant is responsible for brain differences in modern humans as opposed to Neanderthals [17]. Joshy, D, Santpere, G and Soojin, V.Y. support the acceleration of cell-type-specific functional programs as an important feature of human brain evolution [18]. Rickelton, K. et al. identified genes with variation in expression most correlated with brain siz [19]. Cui, X. et al. find the endogenous gene regulatory functions of HARs, potentially contributing to human brain evolution [20]. Pal, A. et al. support that HARs alter the expression of ancestral gene targets shared between human and chimpanzee, influencing brain evolution [21]. Soto, D.C. et al. discover two genes, GPR89B and FRMPD2B, possibly contributing to hallmark features of the human brain [22]. The study of Kaur, N. et al. advances the knowledge of the development and evolution of the ventrolateral pallial (VLp) [23]. Zhang, D. et al. discuss human brain development, exploring its spatial dynamics [24]. Rickelton, K. and Babbitt, C. C. understand the evolution of astrocytes across primates, focusing on gene expression evolution and gene regulation in astrocytes [25]. Raza, R. Z., Nazir, F. and Abbasi, A.A. build a perspective on an already determined set of human accelerated brain enhancers (HABEs) with 34 putative target brain genes for their role in human cognitive enhancements. They conclude that HABE, associated with IRX3, and the Homo sapiens-specific substitution P422L in the IRX3 protein serve as examples of the accelerated evolution of human brain regulatory circuits [26]. Liu, Y., Li, J. and Liu, Q. find that the inactivation of CMAH (CMP-N-acetylneuraminic acid hydroxylase), resulting from natural selection, reduced the level of N-glycolylneuraminic acid (Neu5Gc) in brain tissue. And the low level of Neu5Gc promoted the development of human brain tissue [27]. Soorajkumar, A. et al. synthesize current knowledge of brain cell diversity and highlight key gene markers that define cellular identity and function, exploring how cellular diversity shapes brain function and contributes to disease mechanisms [28]. Chen, Y.-C., Maupas, A. and Nowick, K. developed a software TEKRABber to reconstruct regulatory networks of TEs (transposable elements) and KRAB-ZNFs (TE silencing factors KRAB zinc finger genes) during human brain evolution. They discovered that the human brain displays a notably denser TE:KRAB-ZNF network compared to NHPs, particularly for more recently evolved TEs and KRAB-ZNFs [29]. Liu, J. et al. find that small changes in regulatory DNA can directly affect critical signalling pathways to modulate human brain development, uncovering new functions of HARs (Human accelerated regions) as key regulatory elements crucial for the expansion and complexity of the human cerebral cortex [30]. Caglayan, E. and Konopka, G. use DNA sequence substitutions within cellularly resolved GREs (gene regulatory elements) to gain insight into human brain evolution, and identify ancestral evolutionary patterns of the human brain epigenome at cellular resolution [31]. Yoo, D.A. et al. present complete sequencing of ape genomes for their study of evolution of humans and our closest living ape relatives [32]. In their discussion, the expansion, contraction and restructuring of SDs lead to concurrent gene innovation and chromosomal structural changes [32]. In the case of humans, three gene family expansions, namely NOTCH2NL, SRGAP2C and TBC1D3, have been functionally implicated over the past decade in the expansion of the frontal cortex of the human brain [32].

All researches and results above are based on biological chemistry.

Quantum biology’s origins are often traced back to 1944 and the publication of Erwin Schrödinger’s famous book, What is Life? [33] [34]. In the book, the author declares three results. Firstly, the durability or permanence of genes is unexplainable by classical physics, but is explicable by quantum theory. The mechanism of heredity is closely related to, or founded on, the very basis of quantum theory. Secondly, it seems possible to point out in a more direct manner the connection between “quantum jumps” and mutations of genes. Thirdly, new physical laws are expected in the organism, and the new physical principle is nothing else than the principle of quantum theory over again [33]. Quantum biology is a field of research that applies quantum theory to understand biology, and has unique contributions to life science. Researchers made contributions to quantum biology [33]-[47]. Davies, P. reviewed researches suggesting that living systems process information quantum mechanically, and life will eventually be created as a by-product of quantum information processing and nanotechnology [34]. McFadden, J. developed an evolution theory resorting to quantum tunnelling in base pair formation [35]. McFadden, J. and Al-Khalili, J. reviewed the origin and development of quantum biology, arguing that some of the insights of those quantum pioneers of the early twentieth century, including Erwin Schrödinger, remain relevant to our understanding of quantum biology today [36]. The result of Dikshit, B. shows that life originates out of establishment of quantum coherence in a group of inanimate particles [37]. Tuszynski, J. A. discussed quantum consciousness, commented, based on quantum theory, on the merits, challenging issues and possible developments of the hypotheses suggested to introduce a scientific basis to consciousness theory [38]. Kim, Y. et al. reviewed the progress in quantum biology, concerning the areas of enzyme-catalysed reactions, photosynthesis, spin-dependent reactions, DNA, fluorescent proteins and ion channels, and discussed questions, challenges, expecting further development of quantum biology [39]. Ogryzko, V. V. and McFadden, J. & Al-Khalili, J. study directed or adaptive mutations, based on the principles of quantum theory [40] [41]. Pullman, B. reviewed developments in the quantum mechanical researches on the electrical structure of the nucleic acids [42]. Steele, R. H. discussed the quantization of simple systems in quantum theory, introducing quantum physics into biology in an intuitive way [43]. Wu, L.-A., Wu, S.S. and Segal, D. demonstrated a universal DNA breathing dynamics by means of an approximate method in quantum mechanics [44]. Ruggiero, M. and Pacini, S. discuss quantum processes of DNA that have played a role in the evolution of the human brain and consciousness [45]. Zhao, J. established the System of Bio-Quantum Genetics; proposed the Bio-Quantum Genetic Model of Plant Heredity, theoretically proving Mendel’s results of experiments on plant hybrids; suggested the Bio-Quantum Genetic Model of Human Genetics, explaining human normal inheriting, reversion and atavism [46]. Zhao, J. discussed DNA forming and replicating process within the theoretical framework of the System of Bio-Quantum Genetics established in [46] and reached the conclusion that DNA forming and replicating is a process of bio-quantum entangling, de-entangling and re-entangling [47].

In the present paper, I apply quantum mechanics to understand evolution of the human brain. A typical human family provides the baby with the genes of his/her seniors, and the baby searches the genes of the seniors for his/her parents’ ones and inherits them. In terms of quantum mechanics, an initial bio-quantum state of the seniors’ genes of the human family is established, and the baby searches the initial bio-quantum state of the seniors’ genes for the bio-quantum state of his/her parents’ genes. The search succeeds by using Grover’s fast quantum mechanical algorithm for database search [48]-[51], operating O(N−−√)
 Grover Iterations. Thus, it is found that the ability of the human brain to process bio-quantum information, or, to control bio-quantum computation, strengthens or develops in the human hereditary process. This is bio-quantum evolution of the human brain, or development of human bio-quantum intelligence (human intelligence defined and explored by means of quantum mechanics and quantum computation). Then a law of human bio-quantum intelligence is suggested, consistent with Schrödinger’s expectation [33]. Then bio-quantum selection (selection defined and explored by means of quantum mechanics and quantum computation) is suggested.

2. Modeling Human Family in Terms of Quantum Mechanics

A typical human family consists of n generations of seniors and an offspring baby (n≫1
 ). Every senior is probable to transmit his/her genes to the baby. Because of this, it is possible for the parents of the baby to transmit their genes to the baby (normal heredity), and, it is possible for the grandparents of the baby to transmit their genes to the baby (reversion), and, it is possible for the remote ancestors of the baby to transmit their genes to the baby (atavism). In fact, normal heredity, reversion and atavism are three phenomena of human genetics.

Genes are micro-entities, ruled by laws of quantum mechanics, the physical science of the micro-world. Also, genes are bio-quantum bits, controlled and transformed by quantum computation. Therefore, the underlying physical mechanism of family heredity is quantum mechanical and quantum computational processes. Logically, establishing a bio-quantum theory of family heredity is reasonable.

Using Dirac Notation [52] [53], |F−1,1⟩=|00⋯001⟩
 is the bio-quantum state of the genes of the baby’s parents (the father and the mother); |F−2,1⟩=|00⋯010⟩
 and |F−2,2⟩=|00⋯011⟩
 are the bio-quantum sub-states of the genes of the baby’s grandparents (the two couples of grandfather and grandmother); |F−3,1⟩=|00⋯100⟩
 , |F−3,2⟩=|00⋯101⟩
 , |F−3,3⟩=|00⋯110⟩
 and |F−3,4⟩=|00⋯111⟩
 are the bio-quantum sub-states of the genes of the baby’s grand-grand-parents (the four couples of grand-grand-father and grand-grand-mother);……; |F−n,1⟩=|10⋯000⟩
 ,……, ∣∣F−n,2n−1⟩=|11⋯111⟩
 are the bio-quantum sub-states of the genes of the baby’s ancestors of nth senior generation.

In terms of quantum mechanics, the spectrum of genes of a typical family is a bio-quantum superposition state. The initial probabilities of the sub-states are evenly distributed, because no couple of seniors is, logically and genetically, initially superior to any couple else in the family. Then the initial bio-quantum state of the genes transmitted to the baby by his/her seniors is

|F⟩=1N√∑i=1n∑j=12i−1|F−i,j⟩=1N√(|00⋯001⟩+|00⋯010⟩+|00⋯011⟩+|00⋯100⟩+|00⋯101⟩+|00⋯110⟩+|00⋯111⟩+⋯+|10⋯000⟩+⋯+|11⋯111⟩)
(1)

where

N=2n−1,⟨F−k,m|F−i,j⟩=δkiδmj.
(2)

3. Normal Inheriting by Quantum Computing

The parents are the generation closest to the baby. The baby is borne by his/her parents. Therefore, normal inheriting should be defined as inheriting the parents’ genes rather than genes of the generations far from the baby. To inherit his/her parents’ genes normally, the baby has to search the spectrum of the family genes for his/her parents’ genes and inherits them. Therefore, in terms of quantum mechanics, the parents’ gene state is the unique target. The baby searches |F⟩
 for |F−1,1⟩
 by means of bio-quantum computation. The baby prefers to use a quantum algorithm following Grover’s fast quantum mechanical algorithm for database search , because Grover’s fast quantum mechanical algorithm for database search is the optimal and efficient database search algorithm [48]-[51], the baby finds his parents’ genes, |F−1,1⟩
 ,with a probability of O(1)
 , by searching |F⟩
 in O(N−−√)
 steps (N=2n−1
 ). The details of the algorithm are following.

1) Defining a function f(F−i,j)
 :

f(F−i,j)={1,  |F−i,j⟩=|00⋯001⟩0,  |F−i,j⟩≠|00⋯001⟩
(3)

2) Repeating the following operations (a) and (b) for O(N−−√)
 times (Grover Iterations):

a) Applying the oracle operation:

|F−i,j⟩→O(−1)f(F−i,j)|F−i,j⟩
(4)

where f(F−i,j)
 is the function defined by Equation (3).

b) Performing Grover operation (in terms of inversion about average operation) [48] [49]

(5)

where the diffusion transform D
 can be implemented as

D=WRW
(6)

where W
 is the Walsh-Hadamard Transform Matrix and R
 is the phase rotation matrix [48] [49].

3) Measuring the resulting state of |F⟩
 .

4. Bio-Quantum Computational Speed in Hereditary Process

The (n + 1)th generation of the family inherits his/her parents’ genes by O(2n−1−−−−−√)
 Grover Iterations according to Section 3. This fact means that the family increases its bio-quantum computational speed in its hereditary process.

For example, for n = 100, the bio-quantum computing speed of the baby of 101th generation is O(4×107)
 Grover Iterations per second during pregnancy of 280 days, but for n = 200, the bio-quantum computing speed of the baby of 201th generation is O(4×1022)
 Grover Iterations per second, much bigger than that of the baby of 101th generation, also during pregnancy of 280 days. One Grover Iteration should correspond to one physical operation of the brain.

5. Bio-Quantum Evolution of the Human Brain

A brain is a CPU (central processing unit) of biological information of a person. The human brain controls the bio-quantum computational process and bio-quantum computational speed. The increase, generation by generation, of the bio-quantum computational speed of the family in Section 4 means that the ability of the human brain to control the bio-quantum computation strengthens or develops in the human hereditary process. This is defined as bio-quantum evolution of the human brain.

6. A Law of Evolution of Human Bio-Quantum Intelligence

Human bio-quantum intelligence is developing with the order O(2n−1−−−−−√)
 , where 2n−1−−−−−√
 is defined as Human Bio-quantum Intelligence Root, n is the number of the generations of the seniors of the human family.

7. Bio-Quantum Selection

A human family of (n + 1) generations, whose baby of the last generation is successful to perform O(2n−1−−−−−√)
 Grover Iterations for quantum computation during pregnancy of 280 days, is chosen as a normal family with normal heredity, or, a normal family of normal evolution. This is defined as bio-quantum selection.

8. Discussion

1) Evolution described by Darwin is a process of natural selection.

Human brain evolution is a bio-chemical process in modern evolutionary theories, based on molecular genetics.

Human brain evolution is a bio-quantum computational process in the bio-quantum evolutionary theory suggested in this research, based on quantum mechanics.

2) The bio-quantum evolutionary theory suggested in this research is validated as long as quantum mechanics and quantum computation are valid.

Conflicts of Interest

The author declares no conflicts of interest.

Journal of Quantum Information Science > Vol.16 No.3, September 2026
The Nested Contexts of Quantum Understanding (NCQU) Framework: A Cyberpsychology Perspective on Quantum Cybersecurity Readiness and Organizational Adaptation
Troy C. Troublefield1,2,3orcid
1Department of Cyberpsychology, Capitol Technology University, Laurel, USA.
2Department of Information Technology, Capella University, Minneapolis, USA.
3Department of International Business, International School of Management, Paris, France.
DOI: 10.4236/jqis.2026.163012   PDF    HTML   XML   32 Downloads   248 Views  
Abstract

The emergence of quantum computing presents unprecedented challenges for cybersecurity professionals and organizations, requiring fundamental reconceptualization of cryptographic security paradigms. Despite widespread technical awareness of quantum threats, organizational preparedness for post-quantum cryptography transitions remains inadequate, suggesting that barriers to quantum adaptation extend beyond purely technical domains into psychological and organizational territories. This article introduces the Nested Contexts of Quantum Understanding (NCQU) Framework, a comprehensive theoretical model that integrates cyberpsychology principles with quantum cybersecurity implementation challenges. The NCQU Framework conceptualizes quantum understanding as an emergent phenomenon arising from the dynamic interaction of individual, social, and organizational dimensions operating across temporal, epistemological, and phenomenological spectrums. Through theoretically directed synthesis of research on cognitive biases, professional identity challenges, organizational sense-making processes, and uncertainty tolerance mechanisms, this research illuminates psychological barriers impeding quantum cybersecurity adoption despite available post-quantum cryptographic standards. The framework identifies critical factors including temporal discounting of future quantum threats, expertise entrenchment among classical cryptographers, ambiguity aversion toward quantum-resistant solutions, and organizational culture dynamics that systematically undermine Q-Day preparedness. Drawing on Weick’s sense-making theory, technology acceptance models, and cyberpsychology research on human-technology interaction, the NCQU Framework provides diagnostic capabilities for identifying specific barriers to quantum adoption and targeting interventions across individual, social, and organizational levels. Practical implications emphasize the necessity of integrating psychological readiness with technical implementations, including metacognitive training for paradigm flexibility, scenario-based planning to counteract temporal discounting, professional identity adaptation support, and organizational psychological safety protocols enabling productive engagement with quantum uncertainty. This research contributes to emerging understanding of quantum cybersecurity transitions as fundamentally socio-technical challenges requiring coordinated attention to cognitive, affective, social, and organizational factors alongside cryptographic algorithm selection and implementation capabilities.

Keywords

Nested Contexts of Quantum Understanding, Quantum Cybersecurity, Cyberpsychology, Post-Quantum Cryptography, Q-Day Preparedness, Organizational Psychology, Technology Adoption, Professional Identity, Cognitive Biases, Sense-Making Theory

Share and Cite:

FacebookTwitterLinkedInSina WeiboShare
Troublefield, T. (2026) The Nested Contexts of Quantum Understanding (NCQU) Framework: A Cyberpsychology Perspective on Quantum Cybersecurity Readiness and Organizational Adaptation. Journal of Quantum Information Science, 16, 343-386. doi: 10.4236/jqis.2026.163012.
1. Introduction

The global digital infrastructure is built on cryptographic foundations that are fundamentally vulnerable to quantum computing. Public-key cryptosystems, including Rivest-Shamir-Adleman (RSA), Elliptic Curve Cryptography (ECC), and Diffie-Hellman protocols, secure financial transactions, government communications, healthcare records, and critical infrastructure control systems worldwide [1] [2]. These cryptographic systems derive security from mathematical problems, integer factorization, and discrete logarithm computation, which classical computers cannot solve efficiently within practical timeframes. However, quantum computers operating through fundamentally different computational principles can solve these problems exponentially faster using Shor’s algorithm, rendering current cryptographic protections obsolete [3] [4]. This impending cryptographic discontinuity presents not merely a technical challenge but a complex socio-technical transition requiring fundamental reconceptualization of security paradigms, organizational processes, and professional competencies.

Q-Day, the threshold moment when quantum computers achieve sufficient scale and stability to execute Shor’s algorithm against real-world cryptographic implementations, represents an unprecedented convergence of technological capability and psychological uncertainty in cybersecurity history [5] [6]. Unlike gradual technological transitions, Q-Day constitutes a discontinuous shift in which security assumptions that have maintained decades of cryptographic practice collapse simultaneously across countless systems and organizations. Estimates for Q-Day arrival varies considerably, with projections ranging from 10 to 30 years, depending on quantum hardware advancement trajectories and error-correction breakthroughs [3] [5]. This temporal uncertainty, combined with the paradigmatic nature of quantum computing, poses distinctive psychological challenges for security professionals and organizations seeking to prepare for threats with indeterminate timelines for manifestation.

[7] completed its post-quantum cryptography standardization process in 2024, establishing quantum-resistant algorithms including CRYSTALS-Kyber for key encapsulation and CRYSTALS-Di-lithium for digital signatures [7]. These standardized algorithms provide technically viable alternatives to quantum-vulnerable cryptosystems, enabling organizations to begin transitioning toward quantum-resistant security architectures. However, despite widespread awareness of quantum threats within cybersecurity communities and the availability of standardized post-quantum alternatives, organizational adoption of post-quantum standards remains early-stage and uneven, with available assessments suggesting that a substantial portion of organizations continue to operate cryptographic systems known to be vulnerable to quantum attack while deferring systematic migration to quantum-resistant implementations [8] [9].

This awareness-action disconnect, coupled with high quantum threat recognition and inadequate preparedness behaviors, suggests that Q-Day readiness challenges transcend purely technical domains. Security professionals widely acknowledge quantum vulnerabilities, understand the implications of Shor’s algorithm, and recognize the necessity of post-quantum cryptography, yet organizational implementation remains absent or minimal [6] [10]. This pattern indicates that barriers to quantum adaptation operate substantially in psychological and organizational rather than purely technical territories. Organizations exhibit predictable patterns of denial, deferral, and rationalization when confronting quantum threats with uncertain timelines, manifesting cognitive biases that systematically undermine appropriate preparation despite rational threat acknowledgment [5] [11].

Cyberpsychology, the study of human-technology interaction and psychological processes in digital environments, provides essential frameworks for understanding this preparation paradox [12] [13]. The field examines how cognitive biases, threat perception mechanisms, organizational psychology, and human decision-making processes influence cybersecurity behaviors and outcomes. Applied to quantum security contexts, cyberpsychology perspectives reveal that inadequate Q-Day preparation stems substantially from predictable psychological patterns rather than technical deficiencies or resource constraints. Organizations exhibit systematic temporal discounting of quantum threats, preferring immediate operational demands over future preparedness investments [5]. Security professionals experience ambiguity aversion toward post-quantum solutions with uncertain performance characteristics [14]. Classical cryptography specialists demonstrate expertise entrenchment, resisting quantum paradigms that challenge accumulated professional knowledge [10]. Organizations exhibit collective optimism bias, systematically overestimating their quantum preparedness [6].

These psychological dynamics operate not in isolation but through complex interactions across individual, social, and organizational levels. Individual security professionals develop a quantum understanding within social networks that shape the interpretation and validation of quantum information. Social processes unfold within organizational contexts that enable, constrain, and direct quantum sense-making through resource allocation, cultural norms, and leadership priorities. Understanding develops across temporal horizons, connecting past experiences, present challenges, and future implications. Knowledge manifests across epistemological spectrums, from abstract theory to embodied practice. Professionals progress through phenomenological journeys, transforming confusion into commitment. These multiple dimensions interact simultaneously, creating emergent patterns of quantum comprehension that resist reduction to any single factor or level of analysis.

This article introduces the Nested Contexts of Quantum Understanding (NCQU) Framework, a comprehensive theoretical model integrating cyberpsychology principles with quantum cybersecurity implementation challenges. The NCQU Framework conceptualizes quantum understanding as emerging from nested individual, social, and organizational contexts, operating across temporal, epistemological, and phenomenological dimensions, and developing through identifiable developmental progressions. The framework provides systematic guidance for investigating the complex processes by which abstract quantum knowledge transforms into practical professional competence, illuminating the psychological barriers that impede quantum adoption despite technical awareness and available solutions. By identifying specific dysfunctions across multiple nested contexts and cross-cutting dimensions, the NCQU Framework enables targeted interventions addressing precise gaps in the quantum understanding ecosystem.

Before proceeding to the framework’s theoretical architecture, three constructs central to this analysis require precise definition, as the literature employs them inconsistently. Quantum understanding refers to the cognitive and affective state in which an individual or organization has achieved sufficient comprehension of quantum computing principles and their cryptographic implications to make informed, actionable security decisions, a condition distinct from mere awareness that quantum threats exist. Quantum readiness denotes an organization’s demonstrated operational capacity to execute a post-quantum cryptography transition, encompassing cryptographic inventory completion, algorithm selection, migration planning, workforce competency, and budget commitment; readiness is a measurable state of preparation, not an intention or posture. Q-Day preparedness, by contrast, is a broader construct incorporating both quantum readiness and the organizational resilience factors, cultural adaptability, leadership quantum literacy, psychological safety, and identity-adaptive workforce development, that determine whether a quantum-ready organization can sustain effective security operations through and after the cryptographic transition event. Quantum understanding is the cognitive prerequisite for readiness; readiness is the operational prerequisite for Q-Day preparedness. The NCQU Framework is specifically designed to explain why organizations can possess quantum understanding without achieving readiness, and quantum readiness without achieving comprehensive Q-Day preparedness.

This research addresses three critical questions: 1) How do cognitive biases and psychological mechanisms explain inadequate organizational Q-Day preparation despite widespread technical awareness? 2) What role do professional identity, organizational culture, and social dynamics play in post-quantum cryptography adoption patterns? 3) How can cyberpsychology interventions enhance quantum readiness by addressing psychological barriers alongside technical implementations?

Nature and Scope of This article is a conceptual framework paper grounded in a theoretically directed narrative review of the scholarly literature. It does not employ a systematic or scoping review methodology; no search protocol, eligibility screening matrix, or PRISMA-style reporting was used.
